in &#39;vie&#39;">v</span><span title="/ɛ/: &#39;e&#39; in &#39;dress&#39;">ɛ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/tʃ/: &#39;ch&#39; in &#39;China&#39;">tʃ</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˌ/: secondary stress follows">ˌ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/ɒ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;body&#39;">ɒ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;v&#39; in &#39;vie&#39;">v</span><span title="/ɛ/: &#39;e&#39; in &#39;dress&#39;">ɛ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/tʃ/: &#39;ch&#39; in &#39;China&#39;">tʃ</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">BON</span>-ə-ven-chər, -&#8288;<span style="font-size:90%">VEN</span>-</i></a>; <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Bonaventura da Bagnoregio</i> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="it-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Italian" title="Help:IPA/Italian">[ˌbɔnavenˈtuːra da (b)baɲɲoˈrɛːdʒo]</a></span>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Bonaventura de Balneoregio</i>; born <b>Giovanni di Fidanza</b>; 1221&#160;– 15 July 1274<sup id="cite_ref-walsh_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-walsh-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>) was an Italian Catholic <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscan</a> <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">cardinal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scholasticism" title="Scholasticism">scholastic</a> <a href="/wiki/Theologian" class="mw-redirect" title="Theologian">theologian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Philosopher" class="mw-redirect" title="Philosopher">philosopher</a>.
</p><p>The seventh <a href="/wiki/Minister_General_(Franciscan)" title="Minister General (Franciscan)">Minister General of the Order of Friars Minor</a>, he also served for a time as <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Suburbicarian_Diocese_of_Albano" title="Roman Catholic Suburbicarian Diocese of Albano">Bishop of Albano</a>. He was <a href="/wiki/Canonised" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonised">canonised</a> on 14 April 1482 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_IV" title="Pope Sixtus IV">Pope Sixtus IV</a> and declared a <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_the_Church" title="Doctor of the Church">Doctor of the Church</a> in 1588 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_V" title="Pope Sixtus V">Pope Sixtus V</a>. He is known as the "Seraphic Doctor" (Latin: <i lang="la">Doctor Seraphicus</i>). His <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">feast day</a> is 15 July. Many writings believed in the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a> to be his are now collected under the name <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Bonaventure" title="Pseudo-Bonaventure">Pseudo-Bonaventure</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>He was born at <a href="/wiki/Civita_di_Bagnoregio" title="Civita di Bagnoregio">Civita di Bagnoregio</a>, not far from <a href="/wiki/Viterbo" title="Viterbo">Viterbo</a>, then part of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>. Almost nothing is known of his childhood, other than the names of his parents, Giovanni di Fidanza and Maria di Ritella.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Bonaventure reports that in his youth he was saved from an untimely death by the prayers of <a href="/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi" title="Francis of Assisi">Francis of Assisi</a>, which is the primary motivation for Bonaventure's writing the <i>vita</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He entered the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan Order">Franciscan Order</a> in 1243 and studied at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Paris" title="University of Paris">University of Paris</a>, possibly under <a href="/wiki/Alexander_of_Hales" title="Alexander of Hales">Alexander of Hales</a>, and certainly under Alexander's successor, <a href="/wiki/John_of_Rochelle" class="mw-redirect" title="John of Rochelle">John of Rochelle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> In 1253 he held the Franciscan chair at Paris. A dispute between seculars and mendicants delayed his reception as Master until 1257, where his degree was taken in company with <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Medieval999_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Medieval999-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Three years earlier his fame had earned him the position of lecturer on <i><a href="/wiki/Sentences" title="Sentences">The Four Books of Sentences</a></i>—a book of theology written by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Lombard" title="Peter Lombard">Peter Lombard</a> in the twelfth century—and in 1255 he received the degree of master, the medieval equivalent of doctor.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After having successfully defended his order against the reproaches of the anti-<a href="/wiki/Mendicant_orders" title="Mendicant orders">mendicant</a> party, he was elected Minister General of the Franciscan Order. On 24 November 1265, he was selected for the post of <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_York" title="Archbishop of York">Archbishop of York</a>; however, he was never <a href="/wiki/Consecrated" class="mw-redirect" title="Consecrated">consecrated</a> and resigned the appointment in October 1266.<sup id="cite_ref-Handbook282_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Handbook282-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his tenure, the General Chapter of Narbonne, held in 1260, promulgated a decree prohibiting the publication of any work out of the order without permission from superiors. This prohibition has induced modern writers to pass severe judgment upon <a href="/wiki/Roger_Bacon" title="Roger Bacon">Roger Bacon</a>'s superiors, who were assumed to be envious of Bacon's abilities. However, the prohibition enjoined on Bacon was a general one, which extended to the whole order. Its promulgation was not directed against him, but rather against <a href="/wiki/Gerard_of_Borgo_San_Donnino" class="mw-redirect" title="Gerard of Borgo San Donnino">Gerard of Borgo San Donnino</a>. In 1254 Gerard had published without permission a heretical work, <i>Introductorius in Evangelium æternum</i> (<i>An Introduction to the Eternal Gospel</i>). Thereupon the General Chapter of Narbonne promulgated their decree, identical with the "constitutio gravis in contrarium" Bacon speaks of. The prohibition was rescinded in Roger's favor unexpectedly in 1266.<sup id="cite_ref-witzel_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-witzel-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Coat_of_arms_of_Saint_Bonvanture.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Coat_of_arms_of_Saint_Bonvanture.svg/200px-Coat_of_arms_of_Saint_Bonvanture.svg.png" decoding="async" width="200" height="169" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Coat_of_arms_of_Saint_Bonvanture.svg/300px-Coat_of_arms_of_Saint_Bonvanture.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Coat_of_arms_of_Saint_Bonvanture.svg/400px-Coat_of_arms_of_Saint_Bonvanture.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1068" data-file-height="905" /></a><figcaption>Bonaventure's coat of arms of Cardinal Bishop of Albano</figcaption></figure>
<p>Bonaventure was instrumental in procuring the election of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_X" title="Pope Gregory X">Pope Gregory X</a>, who rewarded him with the title of <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Bishop" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal Bishop">Cardinal Bishop</a> of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Suburbicarian_Diocese_of_Albano" title="Roman Catholic Suburbicarian Diocese of Albano">Albano</a>, and insisted on his presence at the great <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Lyon" title="Second Council of Lyon">Second Council of Lyon</a> in 1274.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> There, after his significant contributions led to a union of the Greek and Latin churches, Bonaventure died suddenly and under suspicious circumstances. The 1913 edition of the <i><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i> has citations that suggest he was poisoned, but no mention is made of this in the 2003 second edition of the <a href="/wiki/New_Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="New Catholic Encyclopedia">New Catholic Encyclopedia</a>.
</p><p>He steered the Franciscans on a moderate and intellectual course that made them the most prominent order in the Catholic Church until the coming of the Jesuits. His theology was marked by an attempt completely to integrate faith and reason. He thought of Christ as the "one true master" who offers humans knowledge that begins in faith, is developed through rational understanding, and is perfected by mystical union with God.<sup id="cite_ref-noone_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-noone-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the year 1434, 160 years after his death, his body was moved to a new church that was considered more fitting. Upon doing so, the head was found to be entirely incorrupt. "The hair, lips, teeth, and tongue were perfectly preserved and retained their natural colour. The people of Lyon were profoundly affected by this miracle, and they chose Bonaventure for the patron of their city. The movement, already on foot, to obtain his canonization received thereby a new and powerful impetus."  However, a century later in 1562, the city of Lyon was captured by <a href="/wiki/Huguenots" title="Huguenots">Huguenots</a>, who burned Bonaventure's body in the public square. In the 19th-century, during the "<a href="/wiki/Dechristianization_of_France_during_the_French_Revolution" title="Dechristianization of France during the French Revolution">dechristianization of France</a>" during the French Revolution, the urn containing the incorrupt head was hidden, after which the church was razed to the ground. The urn has never been recovered.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The only extant relic of Bonaventure is the arm and hand with which he wrote his <i>Commentary on the Sentences</i>, which is now conserved at Bagnoregio, in the parish church of St. Nicholas.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology_and_works">Theology and works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Theology and works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bonaventura_-_Legenda_maior,_MCCCCLXXVII_adi_VI_del_mese_de_februario_e_stata_impressa_questa_opera_-_2360911_ib00890000_Scan00008.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Bonaventura_-_Legenda_maior%2C_MCCCCLXXVII_adi_VI_del_mese_de_februario_e_stata_impressa_questa_opera_-_2360911_ib00890000_Scan00008.jpg/220px-Bonaventura_-_Legenda_maior%2C_MCCCCLXXVII_adi_VI_del_mese_de_februario_e_stata_impressa_questa_opera_-_2360911_ib00890000_Scan00008.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="328" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Bonaventura_-_Legenda_maior%2C_MCCCCLXXVII_adi_VI_del_mese_de_februario_e_stata_impressa_questa_opera_-_2360911_ib00890000_Scan00008.jpg/330px-Bonaventura_-_Legenda_maior%2C_MCCCCLXXVII_adi_VI_del_mese_de_februario_e_stata_impressa_questa_opera_-_2360911_ib00890000_Scan00008.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/Bonaventura_-_Legenda_maior%2C_MCCCCLXXVII_adi_VI_del_mese_de_februario_e_stata_impressa_questa_opera_-_2360911_ib00890000_Scan00008.jpg/440px-Bonaventura_-_Legenda_maior%2C_MCCCCLXXVII_adi_VI_del_mese_de_februario_e_stata_impressa_questa_opera_-_2360911_ib00890000_Scan00008.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2175" data-file-height="3247" /></a><figcaption>Legenda maior, 1477</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bonaventure was formally <a href="/wiki/Canonised" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonised">canonised</a> in 1484 by the Franciscan <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_IV" title="Pope Sixtus IV">Pope Sixtus IV</a>, and ranked along with Thomas Aquinas as the greatest of the <a href="/wiki/Doctors_of_the_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctors of the Church">Doctors of the Church</a> by another Franciscan, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_V" title="Pope Sixtus V">Pope Sixtus V</a>, in 1587. Bonaventure was regarded as one of the greatest philosophers of the Middle Ages.<sup id="cite_ref-robinson_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-robinson-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> His works, as arranged in the most recent Critical Edition by the Quaracchi Fathers (<a href="/wiki/Collegio_S._Bonaventura" class="mw-redirect" title="Collegio S. Bonaventura">Collegio S. Bonaventura</a>), consist of a <i>Commentary on the Sentences of Lombard</i>, in four volumes, and eight other volumes, including a <i>Commentary on the Gospel of St Luke</i> and a number of smaller works; the most famous of which are <i>The Mind's Road to God</i> (<i>Itinerarium mentis in Deum</i>), an outline of his theology or <i>Brief Reading</i> (<i>Breviloquium</i>), <i>Reduction of the Arts to Theology</i> (<i>De reductione artium ad theologiam</i>), and Soliloquy on the Four Spiritual Exercises (<i>Soliloquium de quatuor mentalibus exercitiis</i>), <i>The Tree of Life</i> (<i>Lignum vitae</i>), and <i>The Triple Way</i> (<i>De Triplici via</i>), the latter three written for the spiritual direction of his fellow Franciscans.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The German philosopher Dieter Hattrup denies that <i>Reduction of the Arts to Theology</i> was written by Bonaventure, claiming that the style of thinking does not match Bonaventure's original style.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> His position is no longer tenable given recent research: the text remains "indubitably authentic".<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A work that for many years was falsely attributed to Bonaventure, <i>De septem itineribus aeternitatis</i>, was actually written by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Rudolf_von_Biberach&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Rudolf von Biberach (page does not exist)">Rudolf von Biberach</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1270</span> – 1329).<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For <a href="/wiki/Isabelle_of_France_(saint)" class="mw-redirect" title="Isabelle of France (saint)">Isabelle of France</a>, the sister of King <a href="/wiki/Louis_IX_of_France" title="Louis IX of France">Louis IX of France</a>, and her monastery of <a href="/wiki/Poor_Clares" title="Poor Clares">Poor Clares</a> at Longchamps, Bonaventure wrote the treatise <i>Concerning the Perfection of Life</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-walsh_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-walsh-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Commentary on the <i>Sentences</i>, written at the command of his superiors when he was twenty-seven,<sup id="cite_ref-robinson_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-robinson-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> is Bonaventure's major work and most of his other theological and philosophical writings are in some way dependent on it. However, some of Bonaventure's later works, such as the <i><a href="/wiki/Collationes_in_Hexaemeron" title="Collationes in Hexaemeron">Lectures on the Six Days of Creation</a></i>, show substantial developments beyond the <i>Sentences</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Philosophy">Philosophy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Philosophy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below">
<div class="hlist"><ul><li><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" width="16" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/24px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/32px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="600" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></li><li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/10px-Socrates.png" decoding="async" width="10" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/15px-Socrates.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/21px-Socrates.png 2x" data-file-width="326" data-file-height="500" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Philosophy" title="Portal:Philosophy">Philosophy&#32;portal</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1063604349"><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Catholic_philosophy" title="Template:Catholic philosophy"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Catholic_philosophy" title="Template talk:Catholic philosophy"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Catholic_philosophy&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Bonaventure wrote on almost every subject treated by the Scholastics (see <a href="/wiki/Scholasticism" title="Scholasticism">Scholasticism</a>) and his writings are substantial. A great number of them deal with faith in Christ, God and theology. No work of Bonaventure's is exclusively philosophical, a striking illustration of the mutual interpenetration of philosophy and theology that is a distinguishing mark of the Scholastic period.<sup id="cite_ref-robinson_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-robinson-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Much of Bonaventure's philosophical thought shows a considerable influence by <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">Augustine of Hippo</a>, so much so that De Wulf considers him the best medieval representative of <a href="/wiki/Augustinianism" title="Augustinianism">Augustinianism</a>. Bonaventure adds Aristotelian principles to the Augustinian doctrine, especially in connection with the illumination of the intellect and the composition of human beings and other living creatures in terms of matter and form.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Augustine, who had introduced into the west many of the doctrines that would define scholastic philosophy, was a critically important source of Bonaventure's Platonism. The mystic <a href="/wiki/Pseudo-Dionysius_the_Areopagite" title="Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite">Dionysius the Areopagite</a> was another notable influence.
</p><p>In philosophy Bonaventure presents a marked contrast to his contemporaries, <a href="/wiki/Roger_Bacon" title="Roger Bacon">Roger Bacon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Albert_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Albert the Great">Albert the Great</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas" title="Thomas Aquinas">Thomas Aquinas</a>. While these may be taken as representing, respectively, physical science yet in its infancy, and <a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotelian</a> scholasticism in its most perfect form, Bonaventure presents the mystical and <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Platonizing</a> mode of speculation that had already, to some extent, found expression in <a href="/wiki/Hugh_of_St_Victor" class="mw-redirect" title="Hugh of St Victor">Hugo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Richard_of_St._Victor" class="mw-redirect" title="Richard of St. Victor">Richard of St. Victor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alexander_of_Hales" title="Alexander of Hales">Alexander of Hales</a>, and in <a href="/wiki/Bernard_of_Clairvaux" title="Bernard of Clairvaux">Bernard of Clairvaux</a>. To him, the purely intellectual element, though never absent, is of inferior interest when compared with the living power of the affections or the heart.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_10-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Francisco_de_Zurbar%C3%A1n_012.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Francisco_de_Zurbar%C3%A1n_012.jpg/220px-Francisco_de_Zurbar%C3%A1n_012.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="252" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Francisco_de_Zurbar%C3%A1n_012.jpg/330px-Francisco_de_Zurbar%C3%A1n_012.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/Francisco_de_Zurbar%C3%A1n_012.jpg/440px-Francisco_de_Zurbar%C3%A1n_012.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1256" data-file-height="1438" /></a><figcaption>Bonaventure receives the envoys of the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Emperor">Byzantine Emperor</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Second_Council_of_Lyon" title="Second Council of Lyon">Second Council of Lyon</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Like Thomas Aquinas, with whom he shared numerous profound agreements in matters theological and philosophical, he combated the Aristotelian notion of the eternity of the world vigorously (though he disagreed with Aquinas about the abstract possibility of an eternal universe). Bonaventure accepts the general Christian Neoplatonic doctrine, found in Augustine and Pseudo-Dionysius that "forms" do not exist as subsistent entities, but as ideals, predefinitions, archetypes, or in Bonaventure's words: "exemplars", in the mind of God, according to which actual things were formed. This conception has no slight influence upon his philosophy. Physicist and philosopher <a href="/wiki/Max_Bernhard_Weinstein" title="Max Bernhard Weinstein">Max Bernhard Weinstein</a> described  Bonaventure as a "half-mystic" and wrote that he showed "strong <a href="/wiki/Pandeism" title="Pandeism">pandeistic</a> inclinations".<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Emanationism, exemplarism, and consummation are explicitly listed by Bonaventure as the core principles of theology, all of which are heavily Platonic themes and carry equally Platonic subtopics and discussions but yet are all rooted in the second Person of the Trinity, the Son, incarnate as Jesus Christ, who is the 'principio' of divine exemplars, from which creation emanates from and by which creation is made intelligible and which creation finds as its goal.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Creation is two-fold, expressing the divine truth, the divine exemplar in the Word of God; it "speaks" of that which it is the likeness and subsists in itself and in the Son.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Bonaventure's mature work, the <a href="/wiki/Collationes_in_Hexaemeron" title="Collationes in Hexaemeron">Collationes in Hexaemeron</a>, takes exemplarism, drawn out from his transformation of Platonic Realism, as the basis for vital points of Christian theological dogma: God's love of creation, God's foreknowledge, providence and divine governance, the unconstrained but perfect will of God, divine justice and the devil, the immortality of and uniqueness of human soul, and the goodness and beauty of creation. This also serves as his repudiation of Arab peripatetic necessitarianism and pure Aristotelian identified by the Greek Fathers, if left uncorrected by Plato and Revelation which teach the same thing under different modes.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></p><blockquote><p>Upon [rejection of exemplarism], there follows another [error], that is, that God has neither foreknowledge nor providence, since He does not have within Himself a rational justification of things by which He could know them. They also say that there are no truths concerning the future except that of necessary things. And from this it follows that all things come about either by chance or by necessity. And since it is impossible that things come about by chance, the Arabs conclude to absolute necessity, that is, that these substances that move the globe are the necessary causes of all things. From this it follows that truth is hidden, that is, the truth of government of worldly things in terms of pain and glory. If, indeed, these substances are inerrant movers, nothing is supposed concerning hell or the existence of the devil: neither did Aristotle ever suppose the existence of the devil, nor happiness after this life, as it appears. Here, then, there is a threefold error: a concealment of exemplarity, of divine providence and of world government.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p>Like all the great scholastic doctors, Bonaventure  starts with the discussion of the relations between reason and faith. All the sciences are but the handmaids of theology; reason can discover some of the moral truths that form the groundwork of the Christian system, but other truths can only be received and apprehended through divine illumination. To obtain this illumination, the soul must employ the proper means, which are prayer; the exercise of the <a href="/wiki/Virtue" title="Virtue">virtues</a>, whereby it is rendered fit to accept the divine light; and meditation that may rise even to ecstatic union with <a href="/wiki/God" title="God">God</a>. The supreme end of life is a union in <a href="/wiki/Contemplation" title="Contemplation">contemplation</a> or intellect or intense absorbing <a href="/wiki/Love" title="Love">love</a>; but it cannot be entirely reached in this life, and remains as a <a href="/wiki/Hope" title="Hope">hope</a> for the future.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_10-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Like Aquinas and other notable thirteenth-century philosophers and theologians, Bonaventure believed that it is possible to logically prove the existence of God and the immortality of the soul. In fact, unlike Aquinas, Bonaventure holds that reason can demonstrate the beginning of the world.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> He offers several arguments for the existence of God, including versions of <a href="/wiki/Anselm_of_Canterbury" title="Anselm of Canterbury">Anselm of Canterbury</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Ontological_argument" title="Ontological argument">ontological argument</a> and Augustine's argument from eternal truths. His main argument for the immortality of the soul appeals to humans' natural desire for perfect happiness, and is reflected in <a href="/wiki/C.S._Lewis" class="mw-redirect" title="C.S. Lewis">C.S. Lewis</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Argument_from_desire" title="Argument from desire">argument from desire</a>. Contrary to Aquinas, Bonaventure did not believe that philosophy was an autonomous discipline that could be pursued successfully independently of theology. Any philosopher is bound to fall into serious error, he believed, who lacks the light of faith.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A master of the memorable phrase, Bonaventure held that philosophy opens the mind to at least three different routes humans can take on their journey to God. Non-intellectual material creatures he conceived as shadows and vestiges (literally, footprints) of God, understood as the ultimate cause of a world that philosophical reason can prove was created at a first moment in time. Intellectual creatures he conceived of as images and likenesses of God, the workings of the human mind and will leading us to God understood as illuminator of knowledge and donor of grace and virtue. The final route to God is the route of being, in which Bonaventure brought <a href="/wiki/Anselm_of_Canterbury" title="Anselm of Canterbury">Anselm</a>'s argument together with Aristotelian and <a href="/wiki/Neoplatonism" title="Neoplatonism">Neoplatonic</a> <a href="/wiki/Metaphysics" title="Metaphysics">metaphysics</a> to view God as the absolutely perfect being whose essence entails its existence, an absolutely simple being that causes all other, composite beings to exist.<sup id="cite_ref-noone_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-noone-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bonaventure, however, is not only a meditative thinker, whose works may form good manuals of devotion; he is a <a href="/wiki/Dogmatic_theology" title="Dogmatic theology">dogmatic theologian</a> of high rank, and on all the disputed questions of scholastic thought, such as <a href="/wiki/Universal_(metaphysics)" title="Universal (metaphysics)">universals</a>, matter, seminal reasons, the principle of individuation, or the <a href="/wiki/Intellectus_agens" class="mw-redirect" title="Intellectus agens">intellectus agens</a>, he gives weighty and well-reasoned decisions. He agrees with <a href="/wiki/Albertus_Magnus" title="Albertus Magnus">Albert the Great</a> in regarding theology as a practical science; its truths, according to his view, are peculiarly adapted to influence the affections. He discusses very carefully the nature and meaning of the divine attributes; considers universals to be the ideal forms pre-existing in the divine mind according to which things were shaped; holds matter to be pure potentiality that receives individual being and determinateness from the formative power of God, acting according to the ideas; and finally maintains that the agent intellect has no separate existence. On these and on many other points of scholastic philosophy the "Seraphic Doctor" exhibits a combination of subtlety and moderation, which makes his works particularly valuable.<sup id="cite_ref-EB1911_10-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB1911-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In form and intent the work of Bonaventure is always the work of a theologian; he writes as one for whom the only angle of vision and the proximate criterion of truth is the Christian faith. This fact affects his importance as a philosopher; when coupled with his style, it makes Bonaventure perhaps the least accessible of the major figures of the thirteenth century. This is true because philosophy interests him largely as a <i>praeparatio evangelica</i>, as something to be interpreted as a foreshadow of or deviation from what God has revealed.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonisation">Canonisation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Canonisation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Bonaventure's feast day was included in the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> immediately upon his canonisation in 1482. It was at first celebrated on the second Sunday in July, but was moved in 1568 to 14 July, since 15 July, the anniversary of his death, was at that time taken up with the feast of <a href="/wiki/Henry_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Henry II, Holy Roman Emperor">Saint Henry</a>. It remained on that date, with the rank of "double", until 1960, when it was reclassified as a feast of the third class. In 1969 it was classified as an obligatory <a href="/wiki/Memorial_(liturgy)" title="Memorial (liturgy)">memorial</a> and assigned to the date of his death, 15 July.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He is the patron saint of <a href="/wiki/Gastrointestinal_tract#Lower_gastrointestinal_tract" title="Gastrointestinal tract">bowel</a> disorders.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Bonaventure is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Commemoration_(Anglicanism)" title="Commemoration (Anglicanism)">commemoration</a> on <a href="/wiki/July_15" title="July 15">15 July</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Places.2C_churches.2C_and_schools_named_in_his_honour"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Places,_churches,_and_schools_named_in_his_honour">Places, churches, and schools named in his honour</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Places, churches, and schools named in his honour">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="United_States">United States</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: United States">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/St._Bonaventure_University" title="St. Bonaventure University">St. Bonaventure University</a>, a Franciscan university, in <a href="/wiki/Allegany,_New_York" title="Allegany, New York">Allegany</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_York_(state)" title="New York (state)">New York</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Buenaventura" title="Mission San Buenaventura">Mission San Buenaventura</a> and the City of <a href="/wiki/Ventura,_California" title="Ventura, California">Ventura, California</a>, officially named <i>San Buenaventura</i></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Bonaventure_High_School" title="St. Bonaventure High School">St. Bonaventure High School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Ventura,_California" title="Ventura, California">Ventura, California</a>, United States</li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Catholic Church in Chicago, Illinois</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Bonaventure_Monastery" title="St. Bonaventure Monastery">St. Bonaventure Monastery</a>, a complex of religious buildings, built for the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor_Capuchin" title="Order of Friars Minor Capuchin">Order of Friars Minor Capuchin</a>, located in Detroit, Michigan. <a href="/wiki/Solanus_Casey" title="Solanus Casey">Solanus Casey</a> served here as the monastery <a href="/wiki/Porter_(doorkeeper)" class="mw-redirect" title="Porter (doorkeeper)">porter</a> from 1924 to 1946, meeting visitors at the friary door. The site is a popular <a href="/wiki/Pilgrimage" title="Pilgrimage">pilgrimage</a> site for <a href="/wiki/Metro_Detroit" title="Metro Detroit">Metro Detroit</a> <a href="/wiki/Catholics" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholics">Catholics</a>.</li>
<li>Bonaventure Hall, in Sacred Heart Parish Catholic School, in <a href="/wiki/Patterson,_California" title="Patterson, California">Patterson, California</a>, <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a></li>
<li>Bonaventure Residence Hall, in Viterbo University, in <a href="/wiki/La_Crosse,_Wisconsin" title="La Crosse, Wisconsin">La Crosse, Wisconsin</a>, <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a></li>
<li>Buenaventura Boulevard in <a href="/wiki/Redding,_California" title="Redding, California">Redding, California</a></li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Parish in Huntington Beach, <a href="/wiki/California" title="California">California</a></li>
<li>San Buenaventura de los Tres Arrollos, a lost settlement in the far NE of <a href="/wiki/Custer_County,_Colorado" title="Custer County, Colorado">Custer County, Colorado</a></li>
<li>San Buenaventura de Cochiti built in 1628 in [Pueblo de Cochiti, New Mexico] who remains the patron saint for Native American Village who celebrate on 14 July</li>
<li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://stbonaventure-stbenedictthemoor-queens.org/">St. Bonaventure-St. Benedict the Moor (combined) parish, Jamaica, New York</a></li>
<li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://stbonspaterson.org/">St. Bonaventure Church</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Paterson,_New_Jersey" title="Paterson, New Jersey">Paterson, New Jersey</a>.</li>
<li>San Jose St. Bonaventure Hospital, a fictional hospital that serves as a setting in American TV show <i><a href="/wiki/The_Good_Doctor_(American_TV_series)" title="The Good Doctor (American TV series)">The Good Doctor</a></i></li>
<li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://stbonaventurechurch.com/">St. Bonaventure Catholic Church, Davie, Florida</a></li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Catholic Church, elementary school, and cemetery, <a href="/wiki/Columbus,_Nebraska" title="Columbus, Nebraska">Columbus, Nebraska</a></li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Church, in <a href="/wiki/Allegany,_New_York" title="Allegany, New York">Allegany, New York</a></li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Church, in <a href="/wiki/Glenshaw,_Pennsylvania" title="Glenshaw, Pennsylvania">Glenshaw, Pennsylvania</a></li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Catholic Community and Church, in <a href="/wiki/Bloomington,_Minnesota" title="Bloomington, Minnesota">Bloomington, Minnesota</a></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Canada">Canada</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Canada">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>The town of <a href="/wiki/Bonaventure,_Quebec" title="Bonaventure, Quebec">Bonaventure</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a></li>

<li><a href="/wiki/Bonaventure_Island" title="Bonaventure Island">Bonaventure Island</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Bonaventure_River" title="Bonaventure River">Bonaventure River</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Gasp%C3%A9_Peninsula" title="Gaspé Peninsula">Gaspé Peninsula</a> Region of Quebec</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Bonaventure%27s_College" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Bonaventure&#39;s College">St. Bonaventure's College</a>, a private Roman Catholic school, in <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="Newfoundland and Labrador">Newfoundland and Labrador</a>, Canada</li>
<li>St Bonaventure Catholic School, at <a href="/wiki/Edwards_Gardens" title="Edwards Gardens">Edwards Gardens</a>, <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ontario" title="Ontario">Ontario</a>, Canada</li>

</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Philippines">Philippines</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Philippines">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>St. Bonaventure Parish, Mauban, Quezon is the oldest settlement in the Philippines to have been placed under the protection of El Serafico Padre Doctor San Buenaventura in 1647. It is recorded in the writings of Fray Huertas that in 1759 an unknown man wearing the colors of San Buenaventura defended the town from a moro attack. The people of Mauban have since regarded this as a miracle of their Santo Patron. The largest bell in Mauban that was recast in 1843 is named after San Buenaventura and is rung during the Consecration, Angelus and Plegaria.</li>
<li>St. Bonaventure chapel or Capilla de San Buenaventura in St. John the Baptist Parish, Liliw, Laguna, Philippines, erected in honor of the Seraphic Doctor, San Buenaventura because of the 1664 miracle were tears of blood were seen flowing from the eyes of the venerated image, which was witnessed by the Cura Parroco, Padre Juan Pastor and 120 witnesses; in recognition of this miracle, the first major bell in the church of Lilio was dedicated in honor of San Buenaventura</li>
<li>Barangay San Buenaventura, a village in San Pablo City, Laguna, Philippines. Three small chapels can be found within the village in honour of Saint Bonaventura. The oldest chapel and the original image of Saint Bonaventura is located in Purok-3 Chapel</li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Parish, Balangkayan Eastern Samar, Philippines</li>
<li>San Buenaventura, barangay in the Municipality of Buhi, Camarines Sur, Philippines. Has a chapel dedicated to the namesake saint.</li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Chapel in Barangay San Buenaventura, Luisiana, Laguna.</li>
<li>St. Bonaventure Chapel in Barangay San Bueno, Sampaloc, Quezon.</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="United_Kingdom">United Kingdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: United Kingdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/St_Bonaventure%27s_Catholic_School" class="mw-redirect" title="St Bonaventure&#39;s Catholic School">St Bonaventure's Catholic School</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Forest_Gate" title="Forest Gate">Forest Gate</a>, <a href="/wiki/London" title="London">London</a>, England</li>
<li>St Bonaventure's Catholic Church, and Primary School in <a href="/wiki/Bishopston,_Bristol" title="Bishopston, Bristol">Bishopston, Bristol</a>.</li>
<li>St Bonaventure's Catholic Church, in <a href="/wiki/Welwyn_Garden_City" title="Welwyn Garden City">Welwyn Garden City</a>, England</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Latin_America">Latin America</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Latin America">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>The Municipality of <a href="/wiki/Buenaventura,_Colombia" class="mw-redirect" title="Buenaventura, Colombia">Buenaventura</a> on the Pacific Coast of <a href="/wiki/Colombia" title="Colombia">Colombia</a></li>
<li>The cities of <a href="/wiki/San_Buenaventura,_Chihuahua" title="San Buenaventura, Chihuahua">San Buenaventura</a> in <a href="/wiki/Chihuahua_(state)" title="Chihuahua (state)">Chihuahua</a>, <a href="/wiki/San_Buenaventura,_Coahuila" title="San Buenaventura, Coahuila">San Buenaventura</a> in <a href="/wiki/Coahuila" title="Coahuila">Coahuila</a>, and <a href="/wiki/San_Buenaventura,_State_of_Mexico" class="mw-redirect" title="San Buenaventura, State of Mexico">San Buenaventura</a> in the <a href="/wiki/State_of_Mexico" title="State of Mexico">state of Mexico</a>, all in <a href="/wiki/Mexico" title="Mexico">Mexico</a></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Southern_Asia">Southern Asia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Southern Asia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>St. Bonaventure's Church, a 16th-century Portuguese church is situated on the beach in <a href="/wiki/Madh_Island" title="Madh Island">Erangal</a> near Mumbai. The annual Erangal Feast held on second Sunday of January, celebrating the Feast day of St. Bonaventure, attracts thousands of people of all faiths to this scenic spot. The Feast day of St. Bonaventure is celebrated on 15 July every year.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St_Bonaventure%27s_High_School" title="St Bonaventure&#39;s High School">St Bonaventure's High School</a>, a school in Hyderabad, Pakistan</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Europe">Europe</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Europe">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Bonaventura College is a Catholic high school in <a href="/wiki/Leiden" title="Leiden">Leiden</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Netherlands</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Works">Works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Bonaventure&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>Bonaventure Texts in Translation Series, St. Bonaventure, NY, Franciscan Institute Publications (15 volumes):
<ul><li><i>On the Reduction of the Arts to Theology</i>, Translation, Introduction and Commentary by <a href="/wiki/Zachary_Hayes_(theologian)" class="mw-redirect" title="Zachary Hayes (theologian)">Zachary Hayes</a>, OFM, vol. 1, 1996.</li>
